# M3-02 Parallel Phase 6B Profile

- **Status:** M3-02 complete
- **Decision:** PASS — deterministic parallel Phase 6B; serial remains the production default
- **Measured path:** M3 Hybrid / `SegmentedAgentStorage`, dependency-level Phase 6B, explicit caller-owned Rayon pools
- **Host maximum pool:** 12 threads
- **Extended parity:** 60 §9.7 cells × 5 chunk sizes; 300 configurations passed, no cells skipped

## Executive summary

The release benchmark completed all 360 combinations of population, `p_local`, horizon, thread count, and chunk size. At day 0, the median whole-tick speedup across the 180 active-workload configurations was **0.967×** (range **0.818×–1.142×**); no active-workload configuration exceeded the M3-01 Amdahl model bound of about `1.17×`. Phase 6B key generation and global sorting benefited from parallel execution, while validation/slot lookup and level evaluation/writeback/output were slower at their aggregated medians. Level construction is additional work with no serial counterpart.

At the after-100-days horizon, all 18 cells had zero keyed entries in the three measured days. Alive population was zero except `N=100,000, p_local=1`, where one agent remained alive. These rows are retained as **no interaction workload** measurements and do not support a Phase 6B scaling conclusion.

The measured conflict fraction at day 0 stayed between **0.528892 and 0.541324** across the populations and locality values. It remained nonzero at `p_local=1`, where cross-partition interactions were zero. This is consistent with the §10.8 observation that settlement locality does not determine endpoint contention; the benchmark did not show a whole-tick parallel gain from the tested level schedule on this host.

The follow-up found the first zero-keyed day on days 71–76 with only **0.21%–1.28%** of agents alive; the inference is that near-total mortality plus the static, no-rewire `k=8` graph left no eligible live neighbors. On days 1–3, GiveFood found no targets and about half of keyed StealFood entries ended in TheftFailed; measured search/setup was **8–10×** Applied-positive work, with per-entry timing splits approximate due to hook overhead.

## Host and method

- **CPU:** AMD Ryzen 5 9600X, 6 physical cores / 12 logical processors
- **OS:** Windows 11 Pro, build 26200
- **Rust:** `1.98.1-x86_64-pc-windows-msvc`
- **Configuration:** M3-01's copied M2-40 fixed-group-size TOML; only M3 profile, `k=8`, and `p_local` varied. `K=N/200`.
- **Pools:** `{1,2,4,6}` threads for timing; fixed-size pools were built outside timers.
- **Chunks:** `{64,128,256,512,1024}`
- **Options:** metrics/events enabled; snapshots disabled.
- **Command:** `cargo bench --bench m3_02_parallel_phase6b_bench`
- **Total command wall time:** **1,201.4 seconds** (bench-reported time: 1,201.094 seconds)

The benchmark ran 2 warmups and 11 timed samples per cell. Each sample ran three consecutive days with scratch reuse. Serial and parallel runs were interleaved and started from cloned, identical day-0 or day-100 checkpoints; graph, slot projection, runner context, and pool setup were outside each timer. The serial baseline used the M3 serial phase sequence. The opt-in timing wrappers time the same Phase 6B resolver bodies used by the normal runners; the small clock-read overhead is included in whole-tick measurements. For each thread/chunk/horizon configuration, the timed serial and parallel paths were checked against their uninstrumented runner paths by final State, cumulative Metrics/Events digests, and exact daily records. Timed serial and parallel outputs were also compared to each other.

The timing wrappers split Phase 6B into validation/slot lookup, key generation, global sort, dependency-level build, and evaluation/writeback/output. Serial key generation includes keyed-entry staging; serial global sort includes zero-target ordering; parallel validation/slot lookup includes indexed staging. Therefore those stage ratios compare the corresponding pipeline stages, not isolated instructions. Serial has no dependency-level build; its level-build entry is `—`.

Before timing each horizon, a separate three-day workload pass recorded alive population, daily keyed entries, cross-partition interactions, §9.9 conflict fraction and mean contention, and dependency-level counts. It used the same production M3 phase path and a caller-owned one-thread pool for the level statistics.

## Whole-tick results

Values are median µs/tick over the 20 thread/chunk settings for each row. “Median speedup” is the median of the 20 paired serial/parallel ratios; the range is the minimum and maximum paired ratio. Each detailed per-configuration result, including MAD and all Phase 6B stage times, is listed in the complete matrix below.

The day-0 maximum paired whole-tick speedup was **1.142×**, below the approximate **1.17×** Amdahl upper bound from M3-01. The greater-than-1.17 ratios in the day-100 range occurred with zero keyed entries and are not treated as Phase 6B speedups.

### Chunk and pool sensitivity

Median day-0 whole-tick speedup pooled across the active cells and other settings:

| Setting | Median speedup |
|---|---:|
| Chunk 64 | 0.968× |
| Chunk 128 | 0.968× |
| Chunk 256 | 0.970× |
| Chunk 512 | 0.963× |
| Chunk 1024 | 0.968× |
| Pool 1 | 0.947× |
| Pool 2 | 0.956× |
| Pool 4 | 0.977× |
| Pool 6 | 0.983× |

Chunk size had little aggregate effect in this workload. More threads reduced the median loss, but the pooled median remained below serial. Individual cells vary; the complete matrix retains every exact pool/chunk result.

The measured key-generation and sort stages improve with population, but parallel validation and evaluation/writeback/output cost more than their serial counterparts in the aggregated active cells. Dependency-level construction adds 15–308 µs/tick in the summarized active rows. These stage results explain why faster key generation/sort did not translate into an active-workload whole-tick gain.

At day 0, keyed volume was the same across `p_local` within each population. Cross-partition volume fell from all keyed entries at `p_local=0`, to about half at `p_local=0.5`, to zero at `p_local=1`. Conflict fraction remained about 0.53–0.54 across that change, matching the earlier §10.8 observation. Mean contention stayed close to 1.19–1.20.

## Extended release parity

The release run compared 100-day digest traces from `run_m3_storage_days` with `run_m3_storage_days_with_parallel_phase6b` using the host-maximum 12-thread pool and each chunk `{64,128,256,512,1024}`. The 60 §9.7 cells were the three-seed N=300 matrix (54 cells) plus N=1,000 and 3,000 with three locality values each (6 cells). All **300** parallel configurations matched the serial State hash each day, daily Metrics and Events records, and cumulative Metrics and Events digests. **No combinations were skipped.**

## §10.8 hypothesis and limitations

**Measured:** conflict fraction varied little with `p_local`, including `p_local=1` where cross-partition interactions were zero. Active day-0 whole-tick median speedup was 0.967×, with no cell above the approximate 1.17× Amdahl bound. Key generation and sort improved, but validation/slot lookup and level evaluation/writeback/output were slower in their aggregate medians. The tested chunk sizes produced similar whole-tick medians.

**Inference:** these results are consistent with endpoint contention and dependency-level structure being more informative than settlement locality alone. They do not demonstrate that dependency-level scheduling improves whole-tick performance on this host; the overhead of validation and per-level dispatch/writeback outweighed faster key generation and sorting in most active cells. A contention-controlled topology experiment could test whether changing endpoint overlap changes that balance, but it is outside M3-02 and requires its own contract amendment.

**Limitations:** the measurements use one Windows host and the M2-40 fixed-group-size workload. Day-100 cells had no keyed interactions, so their timing variance is not evidence about parallel Phase 6B scaling. Whole-tick times include the opt-in stage-timing wrapper; its digest parity was checked against untimed runners, but its clock-read overhead was not separately subtracted. The benchmark does not include the optional round-based reservation schedule.

## Interaction workload decay

The M2-40 configuration was unchanged; the six §10.7 endpoint cells use `k=8`, `K=N/200`, and `p_local ∈ {0, 0.5, 1}`. The serial M3 storage path ran from day 0 through day 100 with metrics/events enabled and snapshots disabled. Each measured day was compared with `run_m3_storage_days`: State digest and exact daily M3 metrics matched. The six final State/Metrics/Events digests are recorded below. Mean food is the mean among agents alive after Phase 9; it is `NA` when no agents remain.

| N | K | p_local | first zero day | alive that day | alive fraction | Give/Steal choices | Give/Steal None intents | measured classification |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|
| 10,000 | 50 | 0.00 | 74 | 50 | 0.50% | 23 | 23 | no_eligible_targets |
| 10,000 | 50 | 0.50 | 73 | 55 | 0.55% | 28 | 28 | no_eligible_targets |
| 10,000 | 50 | 1.00 | 71 | 128 | 1.28% | 53 | 53 | no_eligible_targets |
| 100,000 | 500 | 0.00 | 76 | 207 | 0.21% | 105 | 105 | no_eligible_targets |
| 100,000 | 500 | 0.50 | 76 | 217 | 0.22% | 108 | 108 | no_eligible_targets |
| 100,000 | 500 | 1.00 | 74 | 420 | 0.42% | 179 | 179 | no_eligible_targets |

All six first-zero days had nonzero GiveFood/StealFood action choices, and the number of targeted `None` intents equaled those choices. **Measured classification: no eligible neighbors.** **Inference:** only 0.21%–1.28% of agents remained alive. With a static `k=8` graph and no rewiring, near-total population collapse left almost all graph neighbors dead, so no eligible live neighbor remained. The counters do not identify which target predicate excluded each neighbor. M3-04 dynamic topology is the planned stage that changes the static-topology condition; the M2-40 configuration was designed for short benchmarks, not sustained workloads.

**Inference:** these cells lose keyed Phase 6B work around days 71–76 because near-total mortality combines with static topology to remove eligible live neighbors. Day-100 timings therefore cannot represent sustained interaction workload; conclusions about resolver cost should use the day 0–3 active period.

## Search and setup vs applied interaction cost

The release benchmark command was `cargo bench --bench m3_02_parallel_phase6b_bench -- --interaction-followup`; wall time was **56.968 seconds** (bench-reported work time **48.516 seconds**) on the host recorded above. The same six cells were measured serially for days 1–3 from the day-0 checkpoint. Each sample reused scratch over three days; two warmups preceded 11 timed samples, with timed and untimed hook calls interleaved. Phase 4 candidate time is neighbor filtering plus target draw; “other construction” is the timed full intent generator minus that candidate interval, so it also contains shared validation and canonical output sorting. Phase 6B stage times split validation/slot lookup, key generation, global sort, Applied-positive keyed entries (c), other keyed outcomes (d), and output assembly (e). ZeroTarget entries have no keyed commit interval; their counts are reported separately and their classification is within key-generation staging. Search/setup is exactly (a) + Phase 6B validation/slot + key generation + sort. Work is (c). Values are median ± MAD in microseconds per tick; per-Applied values divide by the mean daily count of positive Applied interactions.

Measured: GiveFood found zero targets in days 1–3: `0 Some / 6,585 None` at N=10,000 and `0 Some / 66,031 None` at N=100,000. Consequently, every keyed entry was StealFood. TheftFailed accounted for `3,742 / 7,421` keyed entries at N=10,000 (**50.4%**) and `36,902 / 73,753` at N=100,000 (**50.0%**); those failed theft entries made no state change.

| 10,000 | 1.00 | 211.700 ± 17.833 | 110.184 ± 2.761% | 113.600 ± 10.433 | 42.337 ± 3.418% |
| 100,000 | 0.00 | 2103.267 ± 161.467 | 76.135 ± 8.027% | 1942.733 ± 411.467 | 39.606 ± 10.384% |
| 100,000 | 0.50 | 2208.333 ± 151.467 | 64.393 ± 13.102% | 1938.000 ± 314.833 | 42.851 ± 5.668% |
| 100,000 | 1.00 | 2332.467 ± 169.833 | 68.802 ± 6.774% | 2010.200 ± 213.400 | 41.717 ± 10.279% |

The measured hook excess is substantial: approximately 64–114% of untimed Phase 4 intent-generation wall time and 40–43% of Phase 6B wall time. Per-target and per-keyed-entry timing splits are therefore **approximate**; the hook's extra clock reads measurably perturb total phase time, and the split intervals do not include all clock-read overhead. Timed and untimed Phase 4 intent vectors, Phase 6B resolution vectors, post-Phase-6B food columns and State digests matched. The full serial runner's per-day State/Metrics and repeated Events/Metrics/State digest traces were also exact across samples.

**Inference:** most measured interaction cost is spent on searching or entries that produce no state change: GiveFood target searches return `None`, and about half of keyed StealFood entries end in TheftFailed. A future optimization could skip candidate search when it can prove the same `None` result (for example, when no agent is below the starvation threshold); that behavior needs its own contract and is not implemented here. The large hook overhead makes the 8–10× search/setup-to-Applied ratio directional evidence rather than a precise cost attribution; the per-entry split is not calibrated.
